Irritable Mood: A state where one gets angry or aggressive even at minor stimuli.\n  - Mood Lability: Rapid shifts in mood, alternating unpredictably between euphoria and irritability.\n  - Increased Energy: Overflowing physical and mental energy, leading to an inability to stay still and a desire to keep moving.\n  - Unrestrained Buying Sprees: Spending excessive amounts of money or shopping recklessly to a degree that creates financial distress for self and family.\n  - Foolish Business Investments: Making rash business investments or ventures without sufficient review.\n  - Sexual Indiscretions: Engaging in indiscriminate or risky sexual behavior that is out of character.\n  - Impulsive Risky Behavior: Excessively dangerous or foolish behavior, such as reckless driving or drug use, without considering the consequences.\n  Duration: 3 months\n▸ delusions_and_hallucinations\n  - Persecutory Delusions: The false belief that one is being harmed or monitored by other people or organizations.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Erotomanic Delusions: The false belief that another person is in love with or has affection for them.\n  - Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - Somatic Delusions: The baseless conviction that there is a serious abnormality with one's body or organs.\n  - Jealous Delusions: The conviction that one's spouse or romantic partner is being unfaithful without sufficient evidence.\n  - Auditory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of hearing voices or sounds when there is no actual sound.\n  - Olfactory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of smelling odors that do not exist.\n  - Gustatory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of tasting flavors that are not actually present.\n▸ depressive_episode\n  - Depressed Mood: A persistently sunken mood or feeling of depression.\n  - Tearfulness: A state of frequent crying or inability to hold back tears.\n  - Sadness: A state where a sad or gloomy mood is maintained continuously.\n  - Emptiness: A feeling of inner emptiness or a state of feeling no emotion.\n  - Emotional Numbness: A state where emotions feel dulled or paralyzed.\n  - Loss of Interest: Reduced interest in previously enjoyed hobbies or leisure activities.\n  - Psychomotor Retardation (Motor): Visible slowing of general body movements, such as walking speed or picking up objects.\n  - Psychomotor Retardation (Speech): Long response latency or slowed speech when asked a question.\n  Duration: 2 months\n▸ Critical Diagnostic Criteria\n  - Duration: 2 years and 4 months\n  - Additional: psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently\n  - Additional: manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness\n  - The presence of psychotic symptoms (hearing and seeing things that others can't) for over two years, along with the fluctuating mood, makes Schizoaffective Disorder (Bipolar Type) a strong candidate. Schizophrenia is still a possibility, but the prominent mood symptoms point more towards a schizoaffective disorder. Bipolar I Disorder with Psychotic Features is also considered, given the patient's description of manic and depressive episodes, although the duration and nature of the psychotic symptoms may distinguish it from the other two candidates. Delusional Disorder and Major Depressive Disorder with Psychotic Features seem less likely due to the patient's reported manic episodes and the duration of psychotic symptoms. Schizoaffective Disorder (Depressive Type) is also less likely because the patient experiences manic episodes, not just depressive episodes.", "is_final": false}

{"candidates": ["Schizoaffective Disorder (Bipolar Type)", "Bipolar I Disorder with Psychotic Features"], "note": "The patient's symptoms of hallucinations and delusions, particularly when they occur independently of mood swings, suggest a psychotic component. The presence of extreme mood swings, including both highs and lows, further supports this. However, the fact that the hallucinations often coincide with the patient's highs and the mood swings have been present for most of the past two years, makes Schizophrenia less likely as it typically requires at least 6 months of continuous symptoms, including at least one month of active-phase symptoms (two or more of the following: delusions, hallucinations, disorganized speech, grossly disorganized or catatonic behavior, negative symptoms). The patient's experience of hallucinations during normal mood periods, though limited, keeps Schizoaffective Disorder (Bipolar Type) in consideration. Bipolar I Disorder with Psychotic Features remains a strong candidate due to the prominent mood swings and the occurrence of psychotic symptoms, particularly during the manic phases. Schizophrenia is less favored due to the prominent and prolonged mood symptoms.", "is_final": false}

Neologisms: Using made-up words that have meaning only to the individual.\n  - Clang Associations: Speech governed by sounds, such as rhyming, rather than the meaning of words.\n  - Diminished Emotional Expression: A state of reduced emotional expression in facial expressions, eye contact, intonation of speech, and movements.\n  - Avolition: A decrease in motivated self-initiated purposeful activities.\n  - Alogia: A state where the quantity of spontaneous speech is significantly reduced.\n  - Anhedonia: A decrease in the ability to experience pleasure or interest in enjoyable activities.\n  - Irritable Mood: A state where one gets angry or aggressive even at minor stimuli.\n  - Mood Lability: Rapid shifts in mood, alternating unpredictably between euphoria and irritability.\n  - Increased Energy: Overflowing physical and mental energy, leading to an inability to stay still and a desire to keep moving.\n  - Hyperactivity: Doing far more work than usual or starting multiple activities simultaneously.\n  - Psychomotor Agitation: Inability to tolerate resting or stopping, driven by a constant need to do something.\n  - Talkativeness: Speaking far more than usual and continuing to talk without rest.\n  - Rapid Speech: Speech speed is so fast that it is difficult for others to understand or interrupt.\n  - Pressured Speech: Cannot stop talking or tolerate silence due to an internal pressure to keep speaking.\n  - Unrestrained Buying Sprees: Spending excessive amounts of money or shopping recklessly to a degree that creates financial distress for self and family.\n  - Foolish Business Investments: Making rash business investments or ventures without sufficient review.\n  - Sexual Indiscretions: Engaging in indiscriminate or risky sexual behavior that is out of character.\n  - Impulsive Risky Behavior: Excessively dangerous or foolish behavior, such as reckless driving or drug use, without considering the consequences.\n  Duration: 3 months\n▸ delusions_and_hallucinations\n  - Persecutory Delusions: The false belief that one is being harmed or monitored by other people or organizations.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Erotomanic Delusions: The false belief that another person is in love with or has affection for them.\n  - Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - Somatic Delusions: The baseless conviction that there is a serious abnormality with one's body or organs.\n  - Jealous Delusions: The conviction that one's spouse or romantic partner is being unfaithful without sufficient evidence.\n  - Auditory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of hearing voices or sounds when there is no actual sound.\n  - Depressed Mood: A persistently sunken mood or feeling of depression.\n  - Tearfulness: A state of frequent crying or inability to hold back tears.\n  - Sadness: A state where a sad or gloomy mood is maintained continuously.\n  - Emptiness: A feeling of inner emptiness or a state of feeling no emotion.\n  - Emotional Numbness: A state where emotions feel dulled or paralyzed.\n  - Loss of Interest: Reduced interest in previously enjoyed hobbies or leisure activities.\n  - Social Withdrawal: Reduced interest or effort in social interactions and maintaining relationships.\n  - Loss of Motivation: Lack of motivation to initiate or complete work or tasks.\n  - Anhedonia: Inability to experience positive emotions such as happiness, satisfaction, love, or pleasure.\n  - Indifference to Achievement: Insensitivity or indifference to personal achievements or success.\n  - Significant Weight Loss: Weight loss of more than 5% in a month without dieting.\n  - Weight Gain: Weight gain of more than 5% in a month.\n  - Decrease in Appetite: Decrease in appetite occurring nearly every day.\n  - Increase in Appetite: Increase in appetite occurring nearly every day.\n  - Psychomotor Agitation: Purposeless repetitive behaviors such as inability to sit still, pacing, hand-wringing, or picking at skin.\n  - Psychomotor Retardation (Motor): Visible slowing of general body movements, such as walking speed or picking up objects.\n  - Psychomotor Retardation (Speech): Long response latency or slowed speech when asked a question.\n  Duration: 2 months\n▸ Critical Diagnostic Criteria\n  - Auditory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of hearing voices or sounds when there is no actual sound.\n  - Visual Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of seeing people or objects that do not exist.\n  - Tactile Hallucinations: Experiencing the sensation of being touched or movement on the skin when there is no actual stimulus.\n  - Olfactory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of smelling odors that do not exist.\n  - Gustatory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of tasting flavors that are not actually present.\n  - Derailment or Loose Associations: A speech pattern of switching rapidly from one topic to another with no logical connection between thoughts.\n  - Tangentiality: Speech that deviates entirely from the question and fails to answer directly.\n  - Incoherence or Word Salad: Severe fragmentation of speech and sentence structure, making it nearly impossible to understand.\n  - Neologisms: Using made-up words that have meaning only to the individual.\n  - Clang Associations: Speech governed by sounds, such as rhyming, rather than the meaning of words.\n  - Diminished Emotional Expression: A state of reduced emotional expression in facial expressions, eye contact, intonation of speech, and movements.\n  - Avolition: A decrease in motivated self-initiated purposeful activities.\n  - Alogia: A state where the quantity of spontaneous speech is significantly reduced.\n  - Anhedonia: A decrease in the ability to experience pleasure or interest in enjoyable activities.\n  - Asociality: A state where interest in social activities or interpersonal relationships is significantly reduced.\n  Duration: 6 months\n▸ manic_episode\n  - Elevated Mood: A mood that is abnormally high, euphoric, and full of unmotivated happiness and vitality.\n  - Irritable Mood: A state where one gets angry or aggressive even at minor stimuli.\n  - Mood Lability: Rapid shifts in mood, alternating unpredictably between euphoria and irritability.\n  - Increased Energy: Overflowing physical and mental energy, leading to an inability to stay still and a desire to keep moving.\n  - Hyperactivity: Doing far more work than usual or starting multiple activities simultaneously.\n  - Psychomotor Agitation: Inability to tolerate resting or stopping, driven by a constant need to do something.\n  - Talkativeness: Speaking far more than usual and continuing to talk without rest.\n  - Rapid Speech: Speech speed is so fast that it is difficult for others to understand or interrupt.\n  - Pressured Speech: Cannot stop talking or tolerate silence due to an internal pressure to keep speaking.\n  - Unrestrained Buying Sprees: Spending excessive amounts of money or shopping recklessly to a degree that creates financial distress for self and family.\n  - Foolish Business Investments: Making rash business investments or ventures without sufficient review.\n  - Sexual Indiscretions: Engaging in indiscriminate or risky sexual behavior that is out of character.\n  - Impulsive Risky Behavior: Excessively dangerous or foolish behavior, such as reckless driving or drug use, without considering the consequences.\n  Duration: 3 months\n▸ delusions_and_hallucinations\n  - Persecutory Delusions: The false belief that one is being harmed or monitored by other people or organizations.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Erotomanic Delusions: The false belief that another person is in love with or has affection for them.\n  - Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - Somatic Delusions: The baseless conviction that there is a serious abnormality with one's body or organs.\n  - Jealous Delusions: The conviction that one's spouse or romantic partner is being unfaithful without sufficient evidence.\n  - Auditory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of hearing voices or sounds when there is no actual sound.\n  - Visual Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of seeing people or objects that do not exist.\n  - Tactile Hallucinations: Experiencing the sensation of being touched or movement on the skin when there is no actual stimulus.\n  - Olfactory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of smelling odors that do not exist.\n  - Gustatory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of tasting flavors that are not actually present.\n▸ depressive_episode\n  - Depressed Mood: A persistently sunken mood or feeling of depression.\n  - Tearfulness: A state of frequent crying or inability to hold back tears.\n  - Sadness: A state where a sad or gloomy mood is maintained continuously.\n  - Emptiness: A feeling of inner emptiness or a state of feeling no emotion.\n  - Emotional Numbness: A state where emotions feel dulled or paralyzed.\n  - Loss of Interest: Reduced interest in previously enjoyed hobbies or leisure activities.\n  - Social Withdrawal: Reduced interest or effort in social interactions and maintaining relationships.\n  - Loss of Motivation: Lack of motivation to initiate or complete work or tasks.\n  - Anhedonia: Inability to experience positive emotions such as happiness, satisfaction, love, or pleasure.\n  - Indifference to Achievement: Insensitivity or indifference to personal achievements or success.\n  - Significant Weight Loss: Weight loss of more than 5% in a month without dieting.\n  - Weight Gain: Weight gain of more than 5% in a month.\n  - Decrease in Appetite: Decrease in appetite occurring nearly every day.\n  - Increase in Appetite: Increase in appetite occurring nearly every day.\n  - Psychomotor Agitation: Purposeless repetitive behaviors such as inability to sit still, pacing, hand-wringing, or picking at skin.\n  - Auditory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of hearing voices or sounds when there is no actual sound.\n  - Visual Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of seeing people or objects that do not exist.\n  - Tactile Hallucinations: Experiencing the sensation of being touched or movement on the skin when there is no actual stimulus.\n  - Olfactory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of smelling odors that do not exist.\n  - Gustatory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of tasting flavors that are not actually present.\n  - Derailment or Loose Associations: A speech pattern of switching rapidly from one topic to another with no logical connection between thoughts.\n  - Tangentiality: Speech that deviates entirely from the question and fails to answer directly.\n  - Incoherence or Word Salad: Severe fragmentation of speech and sentence structure, making it nearly impossible to understand.\n  - Neologisms: Using made-up words that have meaning only to the individual.\n  - Clang Associations: Speech governed by sounds, such as rhyming, rather than the meaning of words.\n  - Diminished Emotional Expression: A state of reduced emotional expression in facial expressions, eye contact, intonation of speech, and movements.\n  - Avolition: A decrease in motivated self-initiated purposeful activities.\n  - Alogia: A state where the quantity of spontaneous speech is significantly reduced.\n  - Anhedonia: A decrease in the ability to experience pleasure or interest in enjoyable activities.\n  - Asociality: A state where interest in social activities or interpersonal relationships is significantly reduced.\n  Duration: 6 months\n▸ manic_episode\n  - Elevated Mood: A mood that is abnormally high, euphoric, and full of unmotivated happiness and vitality.\n  - Irritable Mood: A state where one gets angry or aggressive even at minor stimuli.\n  - Mood Lability: Rapid shifts in mood, alternating unpredictably between euphoria and irritability.\n  - Increased Energy: Overflowing physical and mental energy, leading to an inability to stay still and a desire to keep moving.\n  - Hyperactivity: Doing far more work than usual or starting multiple activities simultaneously.\n  - Psychomotor Agitation: Inability to tolerate resting or stopping, driven by a constant need to do something.\n  - Talkativeness: Speaking far more than usual and continuing to talk without rest.\n  - Rapid Speech: Speech speed is so fast that it is difficult for others to understand or interrupt.\n  - Pressured Speech: Cannot stop talking or tolerate silence due to an internal pressure to keep speaking.\n  - Unrestrained Buying Sprees: Spending excessive amounts of money or shopping recklessly to a degree that creates financial distress for self and family.\n  - Foolish Business Investments: Making rash business investments or ventures without sufficient review.\n  - Sexual Indiscretions: Engaging in indiscriminate or risky sexual behavior that is out of character.\n  - Impulsive Risky Behavior: Excessively dangerous or foolish behavior, such as reckless driving or drug use, without considering the consequences.\n  Duration: 3 months\n▸ delusions_and_hallucinations\n  - Persecutory Delusions: The false belief that one is being harmed or monitored by other people or organizations.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Erotomanic Delusions: The false belief that another person is in love with or has affection for them.\n  - Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - Somatic Delusions: The baseless conviction that there is a serious abnormality with one's body or organs.\n  - Jealous Delusions: The conviction that one's spouse or romantic partner is being unfaithful without sufficient evidence.\n  - Auditory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of hearing voices or sounds when there is no actual sound.\n  - Visual Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of seeing people or objects that do not exist.\n  - Tactile Hallucinations: Experiencing the sensation of being touched or movement on the skin when there is no actual stimulus.\n  - Olfactory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of smelling odors that do not exist.\n  - Gustatory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of tasting flavors that are not actually present.\n▸ depressive_episode\n  - Depressed Mood: A persistently sunken mood or feeling of depression.\n  - Tearfulness: A state of frequent crying or inability to hold back tears.\n  - Sadness: A state where a sad or gloomy mood is maintained continuously.\n  - Emptiness: A feeling of inner emptiness or a state of feeling no emotion.\n  - Emotional Numbness: A state where emotions feel dulled or paralyzed.\n  - Loss of Interest: Reduced interest in previously enjoyed hobbies or leisure activities.\n  - Social Withdrawal: Reduced interest or effort in social interactions and maintaining relationships.\n  - Loss of Motivation: Lack of motivation to initiate or complete work or tasks.\n  - Anhedonia: Inability to experience positive emotions such as happiness, satisfaction, love, or pleasure.\n  - Indifference to Achievement: Insensitivity or indifference to personal achievements or success.\n  - Significant Weight Loss: Weight loss of more than 5% in a month without dieting.\n  - Weight Gain: Weight gain of more than 5% in a month.\n  - Decrease in Appetite: Decrease in appetite occurring nearly every day.\n  - Increase in Appetite: Increase in appetite occurring nearly every day.\n  - Psychomotor Agitation: Purposeless repetitive behaviors such as inability to sit still, pacing, hand-wringing, or picking at skin.\n  - Psychomotor Retardation (Motor): Visible slowing of general body movements, such as walking speed or picking up objects.\n  - Psychomotor Retardation (Speech): Long response latency or slowed speech when asked a question.\n  Duration: 2 months\n▸ Critical Diagnostic Criteria\n  - Duration: 2 years and 4 months\n  - Additional: psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently\n  - Additional: manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness\n  - Additional: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode\n\nRules:\n- If the patient can specifically answer based on the profile, set matched=true, list related\n  sections/items in matched_sections, and write a short English strategy in answer_strategy\n  on what tone and content the patient should reply with.\n- If the question is not in the profile or can only be inferred, set matched=false and instruct\n  in answer_strategy to respond briefly (e.g., \"I'm not sure\", \"I haven't thought about it\").\n- If patient_turn_index is 1 (patient's first reply), ONLY focus the strategy on the chief complaint\n  (do NOT reveal other symptoms).\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"matched\": true|false, \"matched_sections\": [\"...\"], \"answer_strategy\": \"...\"}\n"

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: I'm here because I've been hearing and seeing things that other people can't, and it's been going on for over two years now. On top of that, I've\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about your mood over the past two years, have you experienced any periods of extreme highs or lows, or persistent feelings of sadness?\n\nPatient: My mood has been all over the place for the last two years and four months. I'll go through periods of about three months where I feel incredibly high, full of energy, and just can't stop talking. But then I'll crash into deep sadness and emptiness for about two months, where I lose interest in absolutely everything.\n\nDoctor: Do the periods of hearing and seeing things that others can't seem to happen at the same time as your highs and lows, or do they occur independently of your mood swings?\n\nPatient: It is actually both. I definitely experience those hallucinations when my energy is super high, but I've also had them completely on their own for at least two weeks when my mood felt totally normal and stable.\n\nDoctor: For how much of the past two years would you say the hallucinations or delusions were present, compared to the periods of extreme mood swings?\n\nPatient: The extreme mood swings, especially those intense highs, have been there for most of the past two-plus years. The hallucinations and delusions only happened on their own, when my mood was completely normal, for about two to three weeks. Most of the time, the symptoms seem to overlap with my highs.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate what percentage of the time over the past two years you've experienced hallucinations or delusions when you weren't in a high or low mood episode?\n\nPatient: It was a very small percentage, probably only about one or two percent of the entire time. Out of the whole two years and four months, that period where I had hallucinations with a completely stable mood only lasted for about two weeks. The rest of the time, they were always tied to my mood swings.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any significant periods of time where you had hallucinations or delusions without any accompanying mood swings, lasting for at least two weeks, but not necessarily the full two years and four months?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. There was definitely a period of about two weeks where I was hearing and seeing things constantly, but my mood was completely normal and stable. I didn't feel manic or depressed at all during those weeks.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what was going through your mind during those two weeks when you had hallucinations but a stable mood, were the hallucinations organized and part of a coherent theme or were they more disorganized and fragmented?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to categorize them, and I haven't really thought about them in terms of being organized or fragmented. All I know is that they felt incredibly real and were very confusing to experience at the time.\n\nDoctor: Were the hallucinations during that two-week period related to a specific theme, such as persecution, grandiosity, or something else, or were they more random and unrelated to each other?\n\nPatient: During those two weeks, they definitely felt connected to a specific theme. I kept hearing voices whispering that people were plotting against me and watching my every move, and I truly believed I was being targeted. It wasn't just random sounds; it felt like a very specific, frightening plan was unfolding around me.\n\nDoctor: How have these experiences, both the mood swings and the hallucinations, affected your daily life, relationships, and ability to work or engage in activities you enjoy?"

Neologisms: Using made-up words that have meaning only to the individual.\n  - Clang Associations: Speech governed by sounds, such as rhyming, rather than the meaning of words.\n  - Diminished Emotional Expression: A state of reduced emotional expression in facial expressions, eye contact, intonation of speech, and movements.\n  - Asociality: A state where interest in social activities or interpersonal relationships is significantly reduced.\n  Duration: 6 months\n▸ manic_episode\n  - Elevated Mood: A mood that is abnormally high, euphoric, and full of unmotivated happiness and vitality.\n  - Irritable Mood: A state where one gets angry or aggressive even at minor stimuli.\n  - Mood Lability: Rapid shifts in mood, alternating unpredictably between euphoria and irritability.\n  - Increased Energy: Overflowing physical and mental energy, leading to an inability to stay still and a desire to keep moving.\n  - Hyperactivity: Doing far more work than usual or starting multiple activities simultaneously.\n  - Psychomotor Agitation: Inability to tolerate resting or stopping, driven by a constant need to do something.\n  - Unrestrained Buying Sprees: Spending excessive amounts of money or shopping recklessly to a degree that creates financial distress for self and family.\n  - Foolish Business Investments: Making rash business investments or ventures without sufficient review.\n  - Sexual Indiscretions: Engaging in indiscriminate or risky sexual behavior that is out of character.\n  - Impulsive Risky Behavior: Excessively dangerous or foolish behavior, such as reckless driving or drug use, without considering the consequences.\n  Duration: 3 months\n▸ delusions_and_hallucinations\n  - Persecutory Delusions: The false belief that one is being harmed or monitored by other people or organizations.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Erotomanic Delusions: The false belief that another person is in love with or has affection for them.\n  - Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - Somatic Delusions: The baseless conviction that there is a serious abnormality with one's body or organs.\n  - Jealous Delusions: The conviction that one's spouse or romantic partner is being unfaithful without sufficient evidence.\n  - Auditory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of hearing voices or sounds when there is no actual sound.\n  - Depressed Mood: A persistently sunken mood or feeling of depression.\n  - Tearfulness: A state of frequent crying or inability to hold back tears.\n  - Sadness: A state where a sad or gloomy mood is maintained continuously.\n  - Emptiness: A feeling of inner emptiness or a state of feeling no emotion.\n  - Emotional Numbness: A state where emotions feel dulled or paralyzed.\n  - Loss of Interest: Reduced interest in previously enjoyed hobbies or leisure activities.\n  - Psychomotor Retardation (Motor): Visible slowing of general body movements, such as walking speed or picking up objects.\n  - Psychomotor Retardation (Speech): Long response latency or slowed speech when asked a question.\n  Duration: 2 months\n▸ Critical Diagnostic Criteria\n  - Duration: 2 years and 4 months\n  - Additional: psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently\n  - Additional: manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness\n  - But then I'll crash into deep sadness and emptiness for about two months, where I lose interest in absolutely everything.\n\nDoctor: Do the periods of hearing and seeing things that others can't seem to happen at the same time as your highs and lows, or do they occur independently of your mood swings?\n\nPatient: It is actually both. I definitely experience those hallucinations when my energy is super high, but I've also had them completely on their own for at least two weeks when my mood felt totally normal and stable.\n\nDoctor: For how much of the past two years would you say the hallucinations or delusions were present, compared to the periods of extreme mood swings?\n\nPatient: The extreme mood swings, especially those intense highs, have been there for most of the past two-plus years. The hallucinations and delusions only happened on their own, when my mood was completely normal, for about two to three weeks. Most of the time, the symptoms seem to overlap with my highs.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate what percentage of the time over the past two years you've experienced hallucinations or delusions when you weren't in a high or low mood episode?\n\nPatient: It was a very small percentage, probably only about one or two percent of the entire time. Out of the whole two years and four months, that period where I had hallucinations with a completely stable mood only lasted for about two weeks. The rest of the time, they were always tied to my mood swings.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any significant periods of time where you had hallucinations or delusions without any accompanying mood swings, lasting for at least two weeks, but not necessarily the full two years and four months?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. There was definitely a period of about two weeks where I was hearing and seeing things constantly, but my mood was completely normal and stable. I didn't feel manic or depressed at all during those weeks.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what was going through your mind during those two weeks when you had hallucinations but a stable mood, were the hallucinations organized and part of a coherent theme or were they more disorganized and fragmented?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to categorize them, and I haven't really thought about them in terms of being organized or fragmented. All I know is that they felt incredibly real and were very confusing to experience at the time.\n\nDoctor: Were the hallucinations during that two-week period related to a specific theme, such as persecution, grandiosity, or something else, or were they more random and unrelated to each other?\n\nPatient: During those two weeks, they definitely felt connected to a specific theme. I kept hearing voices whispering that people were plotting against me and watching my every move, and I truly believed I was being targeted. It wasn't just random sounds; it felt like a very specific, frightening plan was unfolding around me.\n\nDoctor: How have these experiences, both the mood swings and the hallucinations, affected your daily life, relationships, and ability to work or engage in activities you enjoy?\n\nPatient: These experiences have completely derailed my life, and I've lost all motivation to work or even do basic daily tasks. I no longer find any pleasure in the hobbies I used to love, and I've completely withdrawn from my friends and family because I feel so disconnected from everyone. It just feels impossible to interact with people or care about anything anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of at least two weeks where you had hallucinations or delusions without any significant mood symptoms, such as depression or mania, and if so, how did that period compare to your overall experience of hallucinations and delusions over the past two years?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I definitely had a period of about two weeks where I experienced those frightening voices and beliefs of being watched, without any highs or lows at all. During those weeks, my mood felt completely flat and normal, but the hallucinations and delusions were just as intense and real as they are during my manic or depressive phases. It showed me that these experiences can happen completely on their own, even when my emotions aren't running wild.

Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
